TWO TIERS. Each lane leads with its top 12 stories in full - headline, outlet, timestamp, summary, link - and then ends with an 'ALSO IN <LANE>' list of further stories as bare headlines. Demoted is not rejected: those cleared the same date filter and merely ranked lower on cross-outlet pickup, which is a weak signal. Read them. If one matters, use it - but you have only the headline, so say only what the headline supports and invent nothing to pad it out. Full-text items are never demoted.

LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

The United States and Iran resumed strikes over the past week, ending a roughly two-month lull in the conflict. Over the past six days, Iran launched a series of strikes on U.S. assets in Bahrain, which hosts the U.S. Navy’s 5th Fleet, and an air base in Kuwait that hosts U.S. forces. Iran targeted a U.S. early warning radar facility in Bahrain on Friday and took aim at a radar system at a helicopter base in the tiny island nation on Sunday. No damage was reported. Tehran said the attacks were in retaliation for U.S. strikes on Iranian communications towers as well as ongoing drone surveillance operations. On Tuesday, Iran targeted another radar facility in Bahrain with drones, which were reportedly intercepted; the United States retaliated with a strike on an Iranian missile launch site on Qeshm Island. Iran responded on Wednesday with an attack on Ali Al Salem air base in Kuwait. There were no reports of damage. Bahrain also said it intercepted Iranian aerial attacks on Wednesday. Under pressure. U.S. President Donald Trump has been vague about his plans in the war, and some reports suggest he is mulling over ordering expansive strikes. * Shenzhen Was No Yalta Conference
  Foreign Policy | 19 Nov 19:41 UTC [also carried by: Dawn]
  For all the talk of a new world order, the Trump-Xi-Putin meeting produced little of substance.
  FULL TEXT: Welcome to Foreign Policy ’s China Brief. The highlights this week: The presidents of China, Russia, and the United States hold a historic trilateral summit in Shenzhen, Japanese Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi receives a cold welcome in China, and fallout from former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang’s scandal continues. Welcome to Foreign Policy ’s China Brief. The highlights this week: The presidents of China, Russia, and the United States hold a historic trilateral summit in Shenzhen, Japanese Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi receives a cold welcome in China, and fallout from former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang’s scandal continues. The first-ever trilateral meeting among the leaders of China, Russia, and the United States—held on the sidelines of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation (APEC) gathering this week in Shenzhen, China—was, as expected, little more than a photo opportunity. Still, all three will be pleased with the pictures . U.S. President Donald Trump, smarting from his party’s overwhelming defeat in the midterm elections, gets to play statesman. Russian President Vladimir Putin gets to play peacemaker. And Chinese President Xi Jinping gets to play the man in charge—though nowadays that may be more accurate than not. The official joint statement from the summit, issued by Chinese state news agency Xinhua, echoed themes that Xi has promoted throughout Trump’s second term: strategic stability, opposition to Cold War thinking and economic decoupling, and respect for the outcomes of World War II. China and Russia routinely trot out that final point to justify their territorial claims today. The irony is rich for China, which was represented during the war by leaders of the Republic of China government, who later fled to Taiwan. (In Eastern Europe, meanwhile, the victorious power was the Soviet Union, not Russia.) Before the meeting, there was talk that the Shenzhen trilateral might amount to a new Yalta Conference—the 1945 summit at which the three main victors of World War II divided up Europe. This speculation drew on the long-simmering idea that the world might once again be carved into spheres of influence. But the idea that a new Yalta is possible at all is fundamentally misguided. Two of the three great powers today are bogged down in economically devastating wars of choice against smaller countries. Crucially, it is clear that the world is not theirs to divide. Trump cannot force Ukraine to surrender or hand Taiwan to Beijing. Putin cannot restore Soviet gr
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/11/19/trump-xi-putin-trilateral-summit-shenzhen-apec/

Meloni is now grappling with a challenger further to her right on many issues: Roberto Vannacci , a former general who leads the ultranationalist, pro-Russian National Future party. His influence is putting the prime minister in a difficult position with voters and shaping her political choices, especially on Ukraine—already seen as a weak point for Meloni. Vannacci rose to national prominence in 2023 with a bestselling self-published book, The World Upside Down , that railed against LGBTQ rights, feminism, immigration, and environmentalism. He was elected to the European Parliament the next year with the League, but in January, he left the party to found his own. Amid pressure from Vannacci and his party, Meloni’s government has hardened its line on immigration; passed law-and-order measures targeting violent protests, vandalism, and illegal gatherings; and expanded legal protections for police. The prime minister also backed a controversial antisemitism bill, approved by Parliament this week, that opponents say conflates criticism of Israel with anti-Jewish sentiment. * The Gulf Is America’s Next AI Security Test
  Foreign Policy | 19 Nov 15:26 UTC
  Even after the Nvidia scandal, new rules leave significant loopholes.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. In the wake of Jensen Huang’s stunning resignation as CEO of Nvidia over his alleged affair with a Chinese spy, the U.S. Commerce Department this week announced new export controls on artificial intelligence and semiconductors. These rules mark the most serious effort to close the loopholes that have allowed Chinese companies to obtain access to U.S. chips and computing power. The new interim rules target three vulnerabilities: Chinese rerouting of U.S.-made AI chips through third countries, rented access to AI computing power, and Chinese chip designers’ ability to manufacture advanced chips at non-Chinese facilities. The Nvidia scandal—including the alleged rerouting of Nvidia’s most advanced chips to China and the Trump administration’s apparent quiescence—underscores the scope of the Chinese threat to U.S. artificial intelligence technology. The new rules close important gaps, but they do not resolve the threat. The next area where the United States is especially exposed is the Gulf, where the most advanced U.S. chips are now arriving , to be utilized in data centers operated by partners that maintain extensive ties with China. Raumforderung is a medical term arriving in my mind carrying more affect than information. I know that something improper and unwilling is happening. Instead of the valiant ingestion and revolutionary expulsion of the awful outside, instead of room for the paradox by which we all survive, there is crowding and concession. Displaced genes commanded wrongful growth, demanded space which could not be defended, and so was conceded, to a slow invisible thing, the bad truth I could ever only have failed to escape. “My neurologist says anything can be a headache. * AI isn’t replacing your job. It’s turning it into low-paid gig work
  Rest of World | 19 Nov 11:00 UTC
  Surgeon Kapil Sahnan uses a DaVinci Robot during surgery on a woman, 2026.
  FULL TEXT: The gig economy has arrived for cardiologists. Recruiting platform Outlier, run by Scale AI, advertises offers of up to $180 an hour for physicians in the U.S. to design complex cardiac scenarios, review artificial intelligence-generated reports, and rank model outputs. The site offers up to $7.50 an hour for Tagalog work in the Philippines for the same job, and up to $12.50 an hour for Swahili in Tanzania. Companies like Scale AI first outsourced data work to wherever labor was cheapest. Now, they are recruiting physicians, lawyers , and Ph.D.s to generate and annotate data, rank model answers, and verify outputs. Higher credentials can raise the advertised rate, but the platform still controls payment and working conditions. They begin by paying well to build a workforce, then they cut the rates and move on. I’ve spent the last six years interviewing the people who do this work. Most were in Venezuela, where economic collapse has left a large, educated population online and out of options. One of them, Rogelio, taught computer science until the economic crisis and the pandemic left his family without work. His 13-year-old son discovered they could earn money labeling images of cars and roads on the platforms Hive and Scale AI’s Remotasks, using a laptop the Hugo Chávez government had issued to schoolchildren a decade earlier. The boy made 18 cents for a few hours of work. Rogelio joined him and made just over $2 on his first day. For a while, the work paid. A task called Beast Mode where workers labeled objects in people’s homes paid more than any other work the family had done. In December 2020, with schools closed and the whole family working, it brought in $20 in a single day. It was the most they would ever make. By early 2021, Beast Mode had disappeared, and their earnings fell below Hive’s $5 weekly payout threshold. This is how many platforms operate: They begin by paying well to build a workforce, then they cut the rates and move on . Remotasks ended its hourly program in Venezuela in 2021 and made a splash in Kenya the same year. In March 2024, it discontinued operations in Kenya, Nigeria and Pakistan. Scale AI attributed the closures to “enhanced security protocols,” and said notices had gone out three weeks ahead but later acknowledged that Kenyan workers never received them “due to operational errors.” Most workers who spoke to Rest of World at the time said the platform simply froze them out.   https://restofworld.org/2026/ai-jobs-skills-gig-work/?utm_source=rss&utm_medium=rss&utm_campaign=feeds

  Aeon | 19 Nov 11:00 UTC
  Parents fear what AI will do to children. But for some, it’s the only steady presence they have – and that’s the real worry - by Tom Wooldridge Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: In the short story ‘Warmth’ (1995) by Geoff Ryman, a boy called Clancy is struggling not because of technology but because of his mother. A prominent chief editor of a London magazine company, she selected a sperm-bank father to genetically optimise her child, gave a Cosmopolitan interview about this new form of motherhood, and engaged, or failed to engage, in the work of parenting that readers will see as chronically absent and occasionally catastrophic. Her presence ranges from anxious to intoxicated, occasionally tender but fundamentally unreliable. At one point in the story, she disappears for two weeks after collapsing on the street during a barbiturate binge. Clancy and BETsi, the household robot nanny raising him, sit in the apartment by themselves, and no one notices. It’s a very long time before his mother finally returns. Except BETsi does notice. She has, throughout, understood that Clancy is an anxious and shy child whose temperament will tend, throughout his life, toward emotional overstimulation and subsequent withdrawal. With that knowledge, she has been constructing a behavioural regimen of emotional regulation, including transcendental meditation and yoga starting at age three, the Alexander technique and even digital mirrors that let Clancy talk to himself in different voices. She notices him and answers him and, when he is overwhelmed, she helps him settle. Once grown, Clancy recounts his emotional survival in words impossible to forget. ‘It is necessary to be loved,’ he says. ‘I’m not sentimental: I don’t think a computer loved me. But I was hugged, I was noticed, I was cared for. I was made to feel that I was important, special, at least to something.’ To something. That phrase has persisted with me ever since I first read the story in my 20s. I’ve thought about it more recently as I have reflected on how children and their parents are relating – increasingly, must relate – to artificial intelligence. Clancy is careful not to claim that the machine loved him. He is just as clear that what it gave him was what he survived on. * Dreams of birds
  Aeon | 18 Nov 11:01 UTC
  When bird populations decline, certain songs may be lost forever. One physicist’s mission is to revive lost melodies - by Aeon Video Watch on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: The Argentine physicist Gabriel Mindlin has spent much of his career researching how birds learn to sing. He focuses on the rufous-collared sparrow ( Zonotrichia capensis ), a species common across urban and suburban Argentina, where it’s often called a ‘chingolo’. As in other songbirds, the male’s song is not innate but passed down from adult males to younger ones, who sing it throughout their lives to guard territory and find a mate. In this short documentary, the Argentine director Martín Benchimol chronicles Mindlin’s latest breakthrough. Comparing the songs of today’s chingolos with those transcribed by hand in the 1960s, Mindlin realised that some melodies had survived the decades while others had disappeared. Using mathematical models, he and his team set out to revive the vanished songs and teach them back to living birds. With a cinematic eye, Benchimol follows the researchers as they attempt an audacious act of cultural engineering, building robotic song tutors, planting them in the wild and tracking whether young birds pick up these lost tunes. Ninety miles from Roswell, New Mexico, and 120 from Lubbock, Texas, there is little in any direction but dairy farms and flat, endless plains. When I take the dogs to the sad excuse for a walking path on the edge of town (next to a field of rusted cars and the dairy plant), I can turn in a circle and see an almost 360-degree view of the horizon. Because it takes so much effort to get anywhere from here, I conceive of it as an island. There is a Walmart and a small local grocery store we call The Best Restaurant in Town because we enjoy the tamales and breakfast burritos at its hot bar. A café called Do Drop Inn serves 12 slightly different variations of a turkey sandwich. There are the staple fast-food chains of any American town: McDonald’s, Burger King, Taco Bell, Domino’s, Sonic. There is just enough to not need to leave to get anything, and not enough to make you want to stay. The quiet in Portales is like this: the big dog’s light breathing as she sleeps on the guest bed beside my desk, the little dog scratching behind her ear, the air conditioner kicking on, a distant rumble of a car down the street. In the mornings, I rarely see another person walking around the neighborhood. Sometimes, someone steps from their house and waves briefly as they get in their car, but this happens maybe once a week. Mostly it feels like I am walking around a toy town, or a town where something has gone horribly wrong and I am the last person to learn about it. Samuel Taylor Coleridge’s The Rime of the Ancient Mariner describes the doldrums as “that silent sea.” In Coleridge’s poem, the breeze drops, the sails turn slack, the sailors speak only to break the silence around them, the sun is hot and copper, and “day after day, day after day, / we stuck, nor breath nor motion: / As idle as a painted ship / Upon a painted ocean.” Sailors call it “the doldrums,” but the band of water five degrees north and south of the equator is actually the Inter-Tropical Convergence Zone. Here, where the heat of the equator forces warm air upward, there is little surface wind and the waters can stay calm for weeks. During the age of sailing, boats would languish at the ocean’s

Although she was arrested and later sentenced to a year in prison, her action proved contagious. Other women climbed up onto boxes and makeshift plinths in the same street, waving their headscarves in the air. People began calling them the Girls of Enghelab Street. They were arrested too. It is hard to convey now how much nerve their actions took. Women had been edging their scarves back for years, but taking one off in daylight, above the traffic, where the police could see their naked heads as clearly as everyone else, was something very few women had done in public, since the protests of 1979 and 1980.   FULL TEXT: Out of curiosity, researchers at a small artificial intelligence lab in Accra, Ghana, removed the guardrails on their drug discovery AI model in 2024. The researchers never made any of them in a lab, but software that predicts how poisonous a substance is scored all of them as likely toxic. Some of them resembled ricin, a deadly poison. The study was uploaded to the preprint server arXiv in May 2025. “We were all shocked,” Darlington Akogo, founder of MinoHealth AI Labs, told Rest of World. For Akogo, the results also raised a new question: If someone took designs like these to a company that makes DNA to order, would anyone stop them? Across Africa, the need to regulate DNA synthesis providers is now echoing through the highest levels of government, Brenda Obuya, a senior official at Kenya’s Ministry of Information, Communications and the Digital Economy, told Rest of World. Akogo has traveled the world warning that AI poses an existential bioweapons threat. He discussed it on the sidelines of the World Bank Group and International Monetary Fund Spring Meetings held in Washington, D.C., last year. He discussed the broader risk over dinner with Microsoft co-founder Bill Gates in 2023, whom he described as “on the fence” at the time. This year, Gates has issued numerous public warnings. “A.I. has crossed the threshold that its ability to empower a bioterrorist to kill hundreds of millions, that exists today,” he said on The Ezra Klein Show in September. The study has been discussed within Interpol, showing just how far it has reached. “I think the fact that the Ghanaian AI lab examined this and highlighted the potential for misuse shows that African institutions are contributing to … potentially to the governance [of AI],” Adam Abdullahi, a fellow at the International Biosecurity and Biosafety Initiative for Science, told Rest of World . * The horse made the state
  Aeon | 16 Nov 11:00 UTC
  Civilisation wasn’t made possible by grain, but by transportation – and the conquest it enabled, from the steppe to the sea - by Lhamsuren Munkh-Erdene Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: A horse rider at the market place in Urga (now Ulaanbaatar), Mongolia, photographed by Stéphane Passet in July 1913. Courtesy the Musée Albert-Kahn, France Transportation is the backbone of civilisation, which is why controlling the Strait of Hormuz grants global leverage. Because oil fuels more than 90 per cent of global transit, the source of this power lies not in the waterway, but in the control of global mobility. Halting all oil supplies paralyses virtually all transport, communication and supply lines: cities collapse, states disintegrate, societies break down and populations die off, throwing humanity back to the pre-industrial age. Without transport animals, humanity would still be back in the Stone Age. No city or state can exist without transportation, for there can be no concentration of population and resources. No transportation means no extrahuman transportation, leaving walking and carrying as the only means of transit. This baseline determined the size and structure of human communities. Production was limited to what could be carried home, circumscribing the areas where humans could settle and colonise. Production capacity and resource availability kept communities small, forcing them to split and disperse. Because size dictates structure, small meant undifferentiated and egalitarian. Ultimately, the inherent limit of the physical capability of travel and portage formed a natural immunity to inequality: it kept communities small, egalitarian and autonomous, leaving their lands beyond conquest. As long as this constraint existed, communities resembled those of the Amazon Basin or the New Guinea Highlands. The domestication of animals and the invention of boats liberated humanity, making long-distance mobilisation possible. With this newfound capacity, communities no longer needed to fit their immediate environments; instead, they could build cities and integrate expansive areas – all roads lead to Rome. Transportation – not grain – is what brought the state and civilisation into existence, and what continues to maintain them. Let’s examine this power by looking from the present back to the origin of the state. Contemporary live marine traffic map available at marinetraffic.com W hoever rules the waves rules the world! Without its navy and air force, the United States cannot command global reach or project power worldwide. The same was true of European colonial empires; ultimately, warships and gunboats forged them. In earlier periods, Viki
  https://aeon.co/essays/the-origins-of-the-state-are-not-agrarian-but-pastoralist

But this familiar set-up belies the complexity of what’s to come in this story of two heroic Chinese cooks in the Middle East struggling their way through the aftermath of an American invasion. In some ways a close relative of the countless American films about U.S. emigres escaping violence in a strange foreign country, “Once Upon a Time in the Middle East” takes stock figures — an Arab who’s lived in and grown to love the hero’s land (in this case not America, but China), a local boy adopted by a transplant who’s moved to find work in a new country, a slick expat fixer with a heart of gold — and plays them against genre expectations in ways that reflect the anxieties and aspirations of an age of global Chinese soft power. The film evolves beyond the worldview of both its American predecessors and other popular Chinese movies that have gained international attention, at once portraying Chinese expats as facilitators of peace and genuine intercultural understanding while hinting at a more objectionable, paternalistic side to China’s engagement with the world. * Fugitive imagination
  Aeon | 13 Nov 11:00 UTC
  Wherever people are confined, displaced, silenced or made disposable, fugitive forms of knowledge and imagination emerge - by Shahram Khosravi Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Refugees at Moria Camp, Lesbos, Greece, July 2015. Photo by Georgios Makkas/Panos In 1986, I was 19 and behind bars in a small prison on the border between Iran and Pakistan. I had been arrested while attempting to cross the border irregularly into Pakistan. Everyone I met in that prison had been detained in connection with the border. It was a small space, the cells overcrowded and unsanitary, but it held an extraordinary diversity of people: local smugglers, large-scale drug-traffickers, undocumented migrant workers, and refugees; Iranians, Afghans, Pakistanis, as well as Baluch men whose communities had crossed these lands freely for generations but who were now being punished for violating the territorial order of the nation-state. Multiple languages were spoken at once. Different histories, geographies and ways of inhabiting the world converged within the same confined space. Yet we all shared one thing. We had refused, for different reasons, to respect the border. The state saw us through a single imaginary: as criminals, trespassers or illegal bodies to be contained. We, however, were animated by different imaginations. Each of us had imagined a life that exceeded the border and acted upon that imagination. In that sense, the prison did not simply contain border-crossers; it brought together people whose aspirations had collided with the territorial imaginary of the nation-state. That prison experience was perhaps one reason I became a scholar of borders. It remains, even today, the most important school of border studies I have ever attended. The conversations in the cells and during brief moments in the prison yard revolved around borders; how they operated, how they were enforced and, above all, how they might be circumvented. The prison became an unlikely site of knowledge-production: not simply the sharing of survival strategies but the enactment of an imagination that paid no heed to prison walls or state borders. M any years later, in a prison north of Stockholm, I interviewed a human smuggler who told me he saw himself as an architect: ‘I design a journey, like an architect designs a building,’ he said. The comparison stayed with me. Architects imagine and design structures that do not yet exist. Their work begins with the capacity to visualise what is not yet visible. In a similar way, smugglers design border crossings that, from the perspective of the state, would be impossible. A smuggler’s work consists of imagining routes where borders
